. On each slice we have the Legendrian braid
\Delta
connecting
n
points on both ends.

For a string
a
on the
i
-th level that intersects the
j
-th diagonal, we consider the
j
-th vertical slice in the Legendrian weave, whose front consists of
n
strands. Denote by
\delta_{a,j,r}
the strand that starts from the
r
-th level on the top of the slice to the
(n-r)
-the level on the bottom of the slice in Figure
63
. Define

\eta_{a,j}=\sum_{r=1}^{i}\xi_{a,j,r}\in H_{1}(\mathfrak{w}_{\beta}^{*},\Lambda_{\beta\Delta^{2}}^{*};\mathbb{Z}).

From the definition, if a string
a
intersects the
j
-th and
j^{\prime}
-th diagonal in the triangulation, then
\eta_{a,j}
and
\eta_{a,j^{\prime}}
are isotopic relative to
\Lambda_{\beta\Delta^{2}}^{*}
.

Figure 63.
The vertical slices in the Legendrian weave
\mathfrak{w}_{\beta}
in grey and the strands
\xi_{a,j,r}
on each vertical slice. Two closed
1
-cycles on
\mathfrak{w}_{\beta}
corresponding to the closed strings on the 1st and 2nd levels are respectively drawn in orange and yellow.

Denote by
\eta_{a}
the homology class in
H_{1}(\mathfrak{w}_{\beta}^{*},\Lambda_{\beta\Delta^{2}}^{*};\mathbb{Z})
associated to the string
a
.

Lemma 6.10
.

Let
\beta\in\mbox{Br}_{n}^{+}
be a positive braid and
\Delta
be the half twist. Then
\{\eta_{a}\,|\,a\in I_{\text{str}}\}
forms a basis of
H_{1}(\mathfrak{w}_{\beta}^{*},\Lambda_{\beta\Delta^{2}}^{*};\mathbb{Z})
, and the subset
\{\eta_{a}|a\in I_{\text{str}}^{\text{cl}}\}
forms a basis of
H_{1}(\mathfrak{w}_{\beta},\Lambda_{\beta\Delta^{2}};\mathbb{Z})
dual to
\{\gamma_{a}|a\in I_{\text{str}}^{\text{cl}}\}
in
H_{1}(\mathfrak{w}_{\beta};\mathbb{Z})
via the intersection product.

Proof.

We first check that
\{\eta_{a}|a\in I_{\text{str}}^{\text{cl}}\}
forms a dual basis of
\{\gamma_{a}|a\in I_{\text{str}}^{\text{cl}}\}
via the intersection product. We need to check that

\left<\eta_{a},\gamma_{c}\right>=\delta_{ac}.

First, suppose
a\in I_{\text{str}}^{\text{cl}}
is on the
i
-th level of the string diagram, and
c\in I_{\text{str}}^{\text{cl}}
on the
i^{\prime}
-th level. Note that according to the description of the 1-cycles
\{\gamma_{c}|c\in I_{\text{str}}^{\text{cl}}\}
, such a cycle
\gamma_{c}
will intersect the slice of the weave at 2 points near the
i^{\prime}
-th blue crossing with opposite signs as in Figure
63
and
64
. Hence

\left<\xi_{a,j,i},\gamma_{c}\right>=1,\,\,\,\left<\xi_{a,j,i+1},\gamma_{c}\right>=-1,\,\,\,\left<\xi_{a,j,r},\gamma_{c}\right>=0,\,\forall\,r\neq i,i+1.

This shows that indeed
\left<\eta_{a},\gamma_{c}\right>=\delta_{ac}
. Hence
\{\eta_{a}|a\in I_{\text{str}}^{\text{cl}}\}
forms a dual basis of
\{\gamma_{a}|a\in I_{\text{str}}^{\text{cl}}\}
.

Figure 64.
The closed 1-cycles on
\mathfrak{w}_{\beta}
that start from a trivalent vertex in
G_{i}\,(1\leq i\leq n-1)
, which correspond to the closed 1-cycles on the plabic fence
\mathbb{G}_{\beta}
on the
i
-the level, intersect the vertical slices at the
i
-th blue crossing.

Now we show that
\{\eta_{a}|a\in I_{\text{str}}\}
is a basis of
H_{1}(\mathfrak{w}_{\beta}^{*},\Lambda_{\beta\Delta^{2}}^{*};\mathbb{Z})
. Consider the exact sequence

0\rightarrow H_{1}(\mathfrak{w}_{\beta},\Lambda_{\beta\Delta^{2}};\mathbb{Z})\rightarrow H_{1}(\mathfrak{w}_{\beta}^{*},\Lambda_{\beta\Delta^{2}}^{*};\mathbb{Z})\rightarrow\widetilde{H}_{0}(T;\mathbb{Z})\rightarrow 0.

We have shown that
\{\eta_{a}|\,a\in I_{\text{str}}^{\text{cl}}\}
forms a dual basis of
\{\gamma_{a}|\,a\in I_{\text{str}}^{\text{cl}}\}
, and thus it suffices to show that there is a backward map

\widetilde{H}_{0}(T;\mathbb{Z})\rightarrow H_{1}(\mathfrak{w}_{\beta}^{*},\Lambda_{\beta\Delta^{2}}^{*};\mathbb{Z})

that splits the exact sequence, and send generators to the set
\{\eta_{a}|\,a\in I_{\text{str}}\backslash I_{\text{str}}^{\text{cl}}\}
. Indeed, consider
s_{i}^{\prime}
to be to be the semicircle in
\mathfrak{w}_{\beta}
around the base point
p_{i}\in\Lambda_{\beta\Delta^{2}}
. This defines such a backward map, and clearly

\xi_{i}^{\prime}\in\mathrm{coker}(H_{1}(\mathfrak{w}_{\beta},\Lambda_{\beta\Delta^{2}};\mathbb{Z})\rightarrow H_{1}(\mathfrak{w}_{\beta}^{*},\Lambda_{\beta\Delta^{2}}^{*};\mathbb{Z})).

Note that
\xi_{i}^{\prime}
is isotopic to the strand
\xi_{i}
on the rightmost slice of the weave relative to
\Lambda_{\beta\Delta^{2}}^{*}
. For
a\in I_{\text{str}}\backslash I_{\text{str}}^{\text{cl}}
the unique half open string on the
i
-th level of the string diagram, since
\eta_{a}=\sum_{r=1}^{i}\xi_{i}
, we conclude that they form a basis of
\widetilde{H}_{0}(T;\mathbb{Z})\hookrightarrow H_{1}(\mathfrak{w}_{\beta}^{*},\Lambda_{\beta\Delta^{2}}^{*};\mathbb{Z})
.
∎

Finally, under the isomorphism in (the proof of) Corollary
6.6
, we show how to identify the cluster
\mathcal{A}
-coordinates in
\mathrm{Conf}_{\beta}^{e}(\mathcal{C})
defined in
[
64
]
with the microlocal merodromies along relative 1-cycles in
H_{1}(\mathfrak{w}_{\beta}^{*},\Lambda_{\beta\Delta^{2}}^{*};\mathbb{Z})
specified in the above construction.

Proof of Theorem
1.2
.

Consider a framed sheaf
\mathcal{F}\in\mathcal{M}_{1}^{\mu,\textit{fr}}(\mathbb{D}^{2},\Lambda_{\beta\Delta^{2}})_{0}
that is the sheaf quantization of a framed local system on
\mathfrak{w}_{\beta}
. By Corollary
6.6
, it is represented by the decorated system of flags

\textstyle{U_{-}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{U_{+}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{s_{i_{1}}}
\textstyle{x_{1}U_{+}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{s_{i_{2}}}
\textstyle{x_{2}U_{+}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{s_{i_{3}}}
\textstyle{\dots\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{s_{i_{k}}}
\textstyle{x_{k}U_{+},}

We show that for a given string
a\in I_{\text{str}}
on the
i
-th level of the string diagram that intersects the
j
-th diagonal, the microlocal merodromy

m_{L(\mathfrak{w}_{\beta}),\eta_{a,j}}(\widetilde{\mathcal{F}})=\Delta_{i}(x_{j}).

Consider the
j
-th vertical slice of the Legendrian weave
\mathfrak{w}_{\beta}
as in Figure
63
. On the bottom of the slice, by Proposition
6.5
, we have a decorated flag represented by

(e_{n},e_{n-1},\dots,e_{2},e_{1}).

the trivialization data of microlocal stalks from top to bottom are given by
\Bbbk e_{n},\Bbbk e_{n-1},\dots,

\Bbbk e_{2},\Bbbk e_{1}
. On the top of the slice, we have a decorated flag represented by the matrix

x_{j}=\left(v_{j1},v_{j2},\dots,v_{jn}\right),

i.e. the trivialization data of microlocal stalks from top to bottom are given by
\Bbbk v_{j1},\Bbbk v_{j2},\dots,

\Bbbk v_{j,n-1},\Bbbk v_{jn}
. Note that
\eta_{a,j}=\sum_{r=1}^{i}\xi_{a,j,r}
. Along the strand
\xi_{a,j,r}
, the parallel transport map is the isomorphism

t_{a,j,r}:\Bbbk\,v_{jr}\xrightarrow{\sim}\Bbbk\,e_{r}.

Whenever there is a crossing where on the left the assigned vectors associated to the flag are
v_{1},v_{2}
and one the right the vectors are
u_{2},u_{1}
, we may always assume that the isomorphism
\Bbbk\left<v_{1},v_{2}\right>\xrightarrow{\sim}\Bbbk\left<u_{2},u_{1}\right>
are given by an upper triangular matrix
R\in\operatorname{GL}_{2}(\Bbbk)
with

R(v_{1})=p_{1}u_{1},\,\,\,R(v_{2})=q_{1}u_{1}+q_{2}u_{2}.

Therefore, under the above convention of trivializations, we may assume by induction that the isomorphism
R_{a,j,12...i-1,i}:\Bbbk\left<v_{j1},v_{j2},\dots,v_{jn}\right>\xrightarrow{\sim}\Bbbk\left<e_{n},e_{n-1},\dots,e_{1}\right>
satisfies

R_{a,j,12\dots i-1,i}(v_{jr})=\sum_{s=1}^{r}t_{a,j,sr}e_{s},\,\,\,t_{a,j,rr}=t_{a,j,r},\,\,\,1\leq r\leq n.

The microlocal merodromy along
\eta_{a,j}
is
t_{a,j,12\dots i-1,i}=\prod_{r=1}^{i}t_{a,j,r}
, so since
R_{a,j,12\dots i-1,i}
is an upper triangular matrix, we can conclude that
t_{a,j,12\dots i-1,i}
is indeed the isomorphism

t_{a,j,12\dots i-1,i}=\det(R_{a,j,12\dots i-1,i}):\Bbbk\,v_{j1}\wedge v_{j2}\wedge\dots\wedge v_{ji}\xrightarrow{\sim}\Bbbk\,e_{1}\wedge e_{2}\wedge\dots\wedge e_{i}.

Write
t_{12\dots i-1,i}=t_{a,j,12\dots i-1,i}
for short. Write
v_{j1}\wedge v_{j2}\wedge\dots\wedge v_{ji}=\sum_{k_{1}<k_{2}<\dots<k_{i}}t_{k_{1}k_{2}\dots k_{i}}e_{k_{1}}\wedge e_{k_{2}}\wedge\dots\wedge e_{k_{i}}
. Take the wedge with
e_{i+1}\wedge\dots\wedge e_{n}
, we can conclude that

v_{j1}\wedge v_{j2}\wedge\dots\wedge v_{ji}\wedge e_{i+1}\wedge\dots\wedge e_{n}=t_{12\dots i-1,i}e_{1}\wedge e_{2}\wedge\dots\wedge e_{n}=t_{12\dots i-1,i}.

However, the left hand side is exactly the
i
-th principal minor of the matrix
x_{j}
, i.e.
\Delta_{i}(x_{j})
. Hence this shows that the microlocal merodromy is the cluster
\mathcal{A}
-coordinates defined by principal minors of matrices.
∎

6.1.3.
Cluster
\mathcal{X}
-coordinates on Bott-Samelson cells

Following Section
6.1
, we discuss the cluster
\mathcal{X}
-variables on
\mathcal{M}_{1}(\mathbb{D}^{2},\Lambda_{\beta\Delta^{2}})_{0}
and show that they are exactly the microlocal monodromies along the 1-cycles in
H_{1}(\mathfrak{w}_{\beta};\mathbb{Z})
. In
[
64
]
, cluster
\mathcal{X}
-variables on the double Bott-Samelson cell
\mathrm{Conf}_{\beta}^{e}(\mathcal{B})
are defined independently and they showed in
[
64
, Proposition 3.43]
that the cluster
\mathcal{X}
and
\mathcal{A}
-variables satisfy the standard duality relation:

Theorem 6.11

(
[
64
]
)
.

Let
(I_{\text{str}},I_{\text{str}}^{\text{cl}},\left<-,-\right>_{\text{str}})
be the intersection quiver associated to the string diagram of
\beta
. Let
\{A_{a}\}_{a\in I_{\text{str}}}
be the set of cluster
\mathcal{A}
-variables on
\mathrm{Conf}_{\beta}^{e}(\mathcal{C})
. Then the set of cluster
\mathcal{X}
-variables
\{X_{a}\}_{a\in I_{\text{str}}}
satisfy

X_{a}=\prod_{c\in I_{\text{str}}^{\text{cl}}}A_{c}^{\left<a,c\right>}.

Our goal is to show that
X_{a}
is the microlocal monodromy along
\gamma_{a}\in H_{1}(\mathfrak{w}_{\beta};\mathbb{Z})
. It follows from Theorem
1.2
that
A_{a}
is the microlocal merodromy along
\delta_{a}\in H_{1}(\mathfrak{w}_{\beta}^{*},\Lambda_{\beta\Delta^{2}}^{*};\mathbb{Z})
. Hence we will prove the theorem by determining the relation between the corresponding 1-cycles. By Lemma
6.10
,
\{\delta_{a}\}_{a\in I_{\text{str}}^{\text{cl}}}
form a basis of
H_{1}(\mathfrak{w}_{\beta},\Lambda_{\beta\Delta^{2}};\mathbb{Z})\subset H_{1}(\mathfrak{w}_{\beta}^{*},\Lambda_{\beta\Delta^{2}}^{*};\mathbb{Z})
. Consider the exact sequence

0\rightarrow H_{1}(\mathfrak{w}_{\beta};\mathbb{Z})\rightarrow H_{1}(\mathfrak{w}_{\beta}^{*},\Lambda_{\beta\Delta^{2}}^{*};\mathbb{Z})\rightarrow\widetilde{H}_{0}(\Lambda_{\beta\Delta^{2}}\backslash T;\mathbb{Z})\rightarrow 0.

We can view
H_{1}(\mathfrak{w}_{\beta};\mathbb{Z})
as a submodule in
H_{1}(\mathfrak{w}_{\beta}^{*},\Lambda_{\beta\Delta^{2}}^{*};\mathbb{Z})
. Then the relation between
\{\gamma_{a}\}
and
\{\delta_{c}\}
is as follows:

Lemma 6.12
.

Under the identification
H_{1}(\mathfrak{w}_{\beta};\mathbb{Z})\subset H_{1}(\mathfrak{w}_{\beta}^{*},\Lambda_{\beta\Delta^{2}}^{*};\mathbb{Z})
, we have

\gamma_{a}=\sum_{c\in I_{\text{str}}^{\text{cl}}}\left<a,c\right>\delta_{c}.

Proof.

Similar to the proof of Lemma
6.10
, we can show that the exact sequence

0\rightarrow H_{1}(\mathfrak{w}_{\beta};\mathbb{Z})\rightarrow H_{1}(\mathfrak{w}_{\beta}^{*},\Lambda_{\beta\Delta^{2}}^{*};\mathbb{Z})\rightarrow\widetilde{H}_{0}(\Lambda_{\beta\Delta^{2}}^{*};\mathbb{Z})\rightarrow 0

splits, where the backward map
\widetilde{H}_{0}(\Lambda_{\beta\Delta^{2}}^{*};\mathbb{Z})\rightarrow H_{1}(\mathfrak{w}_{\beta}^{*},\Lambda_{\beta\Delta^{2}}^{*};\mathbb{Z})
maps each connected component
b
of
\Lambda_{\beta\Delta^{2}}^{*}
to a path connecting
b
and a fixed component
b_{0}
of
\Lambda_{\beta\Delta^{2}}^{*}
.

Denote the image of
b\in\pi_{0}(\Lambda_{\beta\Delta^{2}}^{*})
by
\gamma_{b}
. Then it is obvious that
\left<\gamma_{a},\gamma_{b}\right>=0
for any
a\in I_{\text{str}}^{\text{cl}}
and
b\in\pi_{0}(\Lambda_{\beta\Delta^{2}}^{*})
. On the other hand, for any
b\in I_{\text{str}}^{\text{cl}}
, since
\{\eta_{c}|c\in I_{\text{str}}^{\text{cl}}\}
forms a dual basis of
\{\gamma_{a}|a\in I_{\text{str}}^{\text{cl}}\}
via the intersection product,

\sum_{c\in I_{\text{str}}^{\text{cl}}}\left<\left<a,c\right>\eta_{c},\gamma_{b}\right>=\sum_{c\in I_{\text{str}}^{\text{cl}}}\left<a,c\right>\delta_{cb}=\left<\gamma_{a},\gamma_{b}\right>.

This proves the lemma.
∎

In consequences, it follows from Lemma
6.12
, Theorem
1.2
, and Theorem
6.11
above, that the cluster
\mathcal{X}
-variable
X_{a}
coincides with the microlocal monodromy functions of the microlocal rank 1 sheaf
\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}\in\operatorname{Sh}^{1}_{\mathfrak{w}_{\beta}}(\mathbb{D}^{2}\times\mathbb{R})
along
\gamma_{a}
.

6.2.
Cluster coordinates on the moduli of framed local systems via ideal triangulations and Theorem
1.3

Let
\Sigma
be a smooth surface with marked points and consider the moduli space of sheaves
\mathcal{M}_{1}(\Sigma,\bigcirc_{n})_{0}
for the trivial Legendrian
n
-satellite
\bigcirc_{n}\subset T^{*,\infty}\Sigma
of outward unit conormals around marked points, with acyclic stalks at the marked points. The moduli space of sheaves
\mathcal{M}_{1}(\Sigma,\bigcirc_{n})_{0}
is isomorphic to the cluster varieties
\mathcal{X}_{\operatorname{GL}_{n}}(\Sigma)
of rank
n
framed local systems on
\Sigma
, studied by Fock-Goncharov
[
15
,
30
]
.

First, let us review two classes of cluster
\mathcal{X}
-variables associated to an ideal
n
-triangulation, those from bipartite graphs considered in
[
29
,
30
]
, from non-abelianization maps considered in
[
20
,
21
]
, and those from sheaf quantizations of conjugate Lagrangians
[
65
]
. Then we prove Theorem
1.3
, comparing these two classes of
\mathcal{X}
-variables, at the end of the section.

Remark 6.13
.

Note that
[
15
]
considered cluster coordinates on the variety
\mathcal{X}_{\operatorname{PGL}_{n}}(\Sigma)
of rank
n

\operatorname{PGL}_{n}
-local systems on
\Sigma
. However, we focus on
\mathcal{X}_{\operatorname{GL}_{n}}(\Sigma)
, as cluster
\mathcal{X}
-variables there determine the
\mathcal{X}
-variables on
\mathcal{X}_{\operatorname{PGL}_{n}}(\Sigma)
via a projection map.
30
30

30

In
[
15
]
, there is also a corresponding cluster
\mathcal{A}
-variety
\mathcal{A}_{\operatorname{GL}_{n}}(\Sigma)
, resp.
\mathcal{A}_{\mathcal{L}_{n}}(\Sigma)
, whose coordinates determine the cluster
\mathcal{X}
-coordinates of
\mathcal{X}_{\operatorname{GL}_{n}}(\Sigma)
, resp.
\mathcal{X}_{\operatorname{PGL}_{n}}(\Sigma)
. A similar construction with microlocal holonomies, following in Section
6.1
should likely exist but it has not yet been constructed in the literature. We thus only focus on the cluster
\mathcal{X}
-structure.

6.2.1.
Framed local systems on a surface

The moduli space of sheaves
\mathcal{M}_{1}(\Sigma,\bigcirc_{n})_{0}
, see Appendix
B.4
, can be identified with the following moduli space of framed local systems:

Definition 6.14

(
[
15
]
*Definition 1.2)
.

Let
\Sigma
be a surface with marked points
\{x_{1},...,x_{m}\}
,
\Sigma_{\text{op}}=\Sigma\backslash\{x_{1},...,x_{m}\}
and
\Sigma_{\text{cl}}=\Sigma\backslash\bigcup_{i=1}^{m}B_{\epsilon}(x_{i})
. A framed
\operatorname{GL}_{n}
-local system
(\mathcal{L},\sigma)
on
\Sigma_{\text{op}}
is a
\operatorname{GL}_{n}
-local system
\mathcal{L}
with a flat section
\sigma:\partial\Sigma_{\text{cl}}\rightarrow\mathcal{L}/\mathrm{B}_{+}
. The space
\mathcal{X}_{\operatorname{GL}_{n}}(\Sigma)
is the moduli space of framed local systems on
\Sigma_{\text{op}}
.

A framed
\operatorname{PGL}_{n}
-local system
(\mathcal{L},\sigma)
on
\Sigma_{\text{op}}
is a
\operatorname{PGL}_{n}
-local system
\mathcal{L}
with a flat section
\sigma:\partial\Sigma_{\text{cl}}\rightarrow\mathcal{L}/\mathrm{B}_{+}.
The space
\mathcal{X}_{\operatorname{PGL}_{n}}(\Sigma)
is the moduli space of framed local systems on
\Sigma_{\text{op}}
.

Remark 6.15
.

There exists a natural projection map
\mathcal{X}_{\operatorname{GL}_{n}}(\Sigma)\rightarrow\mathcal{X}_{\operatorname{PGL}_{n}}(\Sigma)
with fiber
(\Bbbk^{\times})^{m}
. The fiber over
(\mathcal{L}_{0},\sigma_{0})
parametrizes framed
\operatorname{GL}_{n}
-local systems
(\mathcal{L},\sigma)
where the flat section
\sigma:\partial\Sigma_{\text{cl}}\rightarrow\mathcal{L}/\mathrm{B}_{+}
is reduce to
\sigma_{0}
under the quotient map
\operatorname{GL}_{n}\rightarrow\operatorname{PGL}_{n}
. Note that the flat section lives in the Cartan subgroup, and
\operatorname{GL}_{n}\rightarrow\operatorname{PGL}_{n}
factors through the corresponding Cartan subgroups
(\Bbbk^{\times})^{n}\rightarrow(\Bbbk^{\times})^{n-1}
. Remarks
6.20
and
6.24
explain that, from the point of view of cluster varieties, the projection map is exactly defined by forgetting the frozen variables.

The following Proposition
6.16
is folklore, see
[
31
]
*Lemma 8.11 for a similar result in a slightly different setting, and we include its proof so it is explicitly available:

Proposition 6.16
.

Let
\Sigma
be a surface with marked points
\{x_{1},...,x_{m}\}
. Let
\bigcirc_{n}
be the union of the inward conormal bundle of
n
concentric circles around
x_{1},...,x_{m}
. Let
\mathcal{M}_{1}(\Sigma,\bigcirc_{n})_{0}
be the moduli space of microlocal rank 1 sheaves in
\operatorname{Sh}^{1}_{\bigcirc_{n}}(\Sigma)
with acyclic stalks at
x_{1},...,x_{m}
. Then there is an equivalence of moduli spaces

\mathcal{X}_{\operatorname{GL}_{n}}(\Sigma)\xrightarrow{\sim}\mathcal{M}_{1}(\Sigma,\bigcirc_{n})_{0}.

Proof.

We fix the following notations. Consider the stratfication of
\Sigma
defined by
\pi(\Lambda_{n})\subset\Sigma
. Write
A_{0}(x_{j})
for the closed disks inside the smallest concentric circle around
x_{j}\,(1\leq j\leq m)
. Write
A_{i}(x_{j})
for the annulus between the
i
-th and
(i+1)
-th concentric circles (from inside to outside) around
x_{j}\,(1\leq j\leq m)
containing the
i
-th concentric circle. Without loss of generality, let
B_{\epsilon}(x_{j})=\bigcup_{i=0}^{n-1}A_{i}(x_{j})
and
\Sigma_{\text{cl}}=\Sigma\backslash\bigcup_{j=1}^{m}B_{\epsilon}(x_{j})
.

For a
GL_{n}
-local system
\mathcal{L}
, a section
\sigma
near
\partial\Sigma_{\text{cl}}
determines a framing
\{s_{1},...,s_{n}\}
near
\mathcal{L}|_{\partial B_{\epsilon}(x_{i})}
for each marked point
x_{i}\,(1\leq i\leq m)
. Therefore, we can consider the flag of
S^{1}
-local systems near each marked point
x_{i}\,(1\leq i\leq m)

0_{S^{0}}=\mathcal{L}_{0}\subset\mathcal{L}_{1}\subset\mathcal{L}_{2}\subset...\subset\mathcal{L}_{n}\cong\mathcal{L}|_{\partial B_{\epsilon}(x_{j})}.

Therefore we can define a sheaf
\mathcal{F}\in\operatorname{Sh}^{1}_{\bigcirc_{n}}(\Sigma)
such that (1) in each small disk
A_{0}(x_{j})
,
\mathcal{F}|_{A_{0}(x_{j})}=0_{A_{0}(x_{j})}
; (2) in the annulus
A_{i}(x_{j})
, the restriction of
\mathcal{F}|_{A_{i}(x_{j})}
is a local system determined by (the pullback of) a
S^{1}
-local system
\mathcal{L}_{i}
to
A_{i}(x_{j})
; (3) finally in
\Sigma_{\text{cl}}
, the restriction
\mathcal{F}|_{\Sigma_{\text{cl}}}\simeq\mathcal{L}|_{\Sigma_{\text{cl}}}
. Locally when we pass the
i
-th concentric circle, we assign the parallel transport map (see Example
5.3
) from
\mathcal{F}|_{A_{i-1}(x_{j})}
to
\mathcal{F}|_{A_{i}(x_{j})}
to be the natural inclusion

\mathcal{L}_{i-1}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{L}_{i}.

Since along the
i
-th concentric circle around
x_{j}\,(1\leq j\leq m)
, the microlocal monodromy is a rank 1 local system
\mathcal{L}_{i}/\mathcal{L}_{i-1}
. Therefore the sheaf
\mathcal{F}
lies in
\mathcal{M}(\Sigma,\bigcirc_{n})_{0}
.

Conversely, consider any sheaf
\mathcal{F}\in\operatorname{Sh}^{1}_{\bigcirc_{n}}(\Sigma)
with acyclic stalks at
x_{1},...,x_{m}
. Then define
\mathcal{L}=\mathcal{F}|_{\Sigma_{\text{cl}}^{\circ}}
where
\Sigma_{\text{cl}}^{\circ}
is the interior of
\Sigma_{\text{cl}}
, diffeomorphic to
\Sigma_{\text{op}}
. We now try to define a flag of
S^{1}
-local systems

0_{S^{1}}=\mathcal{L}_{0}\subset\mathcal{L}_{1}\subset\mathcal{L}_{2}\subset...\subset\mathcal{L}_{n}\cong\mathcal{L}|_{\partial B_{\epsilon}(x_{i})}

along
\mathcal{L}|_{\partial B_{\epsilon}(x_{j})}
for each marked point
x_{j}\,(1\leq j\leq m)
. Note that for any
1\leq i\leq n-1
,
\mathcal{F}|_{A_{i}(x_{j})}
is a locally constant sheaf, and thus come from the pullback of an
S^{1}
-local system
\mathcal{L}_{i}
. We can check that
\mathcal{L}_{i}\,(1\leq i\leq n-1)
defines a flag of
S^{1}
-local systems. Note that along the
i
-th concentric circle, the microlocal monodromy

\mathrm{Cone}(\mathcal{L}_{i-1}\rightarrow\mathcal{L}_{i})

is a rank 1 local system. When
i=0
,
\mathcal{L}_{0}\cong 0_{S^{1}}
, using this fact we can tell that
\mathcal{L}_{1}
is a rank 1 local system (in degree 0) and
\mathcal{L}_{0}\rightarrow\mathcal{L}_{1}
is an injection. Suppose
\mathcal{L}_{i-1}
is a rank
(i-1)
local system (in degree 0), then using the same fact we can tell that
\mathcal{L}_{i}
is a rank
i
local system (in degree 0), and
\mathcal{L}_{i-1}\rightarrow\mathcal{L}_{i}
is an injection. Therefore we can get a flag of
S^{1}
-local systems, which determines the framing around
\mathcal{L}|_{\partial B_{\epsilon}(x_{j})}\,(1\leq j\leq m)
.

These two constructions from framed local systems to microlocal sheaves and back are inverses to each other, and thus we have completed the proof.
∎

Remark 6.17
.

Similarly, one can show that given a collection of braids
\beta=\{\beta_{1},...,\beta_{m}\}
around each puncture, the moduli stack of
\beta
-framed local systems
\mathcal{X}_{\operatorname{GL}_{n}}(\Sigma,\beta)
introduced in
[
31
]
*Section 9 is isomorphic to
\mathcal{M}_{1}(\Sigma,\Lambda_{\beta})_{0}
, which is also referred to as the moduli of
\beta
-filtered local system in
[
65
]
*Section 3.1.

6.2.2.
Non-abelianization map and sheaves over weaves

As has been noticed in
[
10
]
*Section 3.1, the Legendrian
n
-weave of an
n
-triangle is closedly related to spectral covers introduced by D. Gaiotto, G. Moore and A. Neitzke
[
20
,
21
]
, at least on the smooth level. Here we interpret the cluster
\mathcal{X}
-variables on the moduli of framed local systems coming from non-abelianization
[
20
]
using the language of Legendrian weaves and microlocal sheaves.

When defining cluster
\mathcal{X}
-coordinates,
[
20
,
21
]
only use the information of the Lagrangian as smooth branched covers and define the coordinates as monodromies of local systems on that Lagrangian spectral curve. The spectral covers they considered are Lagrangian branched covers in the cotangent bundle of the triangle with exactly
n^{2}
simple branched points, and hence there is a diffeomorphism between these spectral covers and (Lagrangian projections of) the weaves coming from the
n
-triangulation, that intertwines with the projection maps.

Starting from a Riemann surface
\Sigma
with punctures, consider a free Legendrian weave
\widetilde{L}\subset J^{1}(\Sigma_{\text{op}})
, with compactification in
J^{1}(\Sigma_{\text{cl}})
. Then the boundary
\partial\widetilde{L}\subset\partial\Sigma_{\text{cl}}\times\mathbb{R}
determines a Legendrian
n
-braid closure
\Lambda_{\beta}\subset J^{1}(\partial\Sigma_{\text{cl}})
, which, under the convention in Subsection
2.3
, can also be identified with Legendrian
n
-braid closures in
T^{*,\infty}\Sigma
as satellites around the inward unit conormals of small disks around the punctures.

Let
\mathcal{X}_{\mathfrak{w}}=H^{1}(\mathfrak{w};\Bbbk^{\times})
be the moduli space of rank 1 local systems on the Legendrian weave
\mathfrak{w}
.
31
31

31

In the literature, for example
[
20
]
*Section 10,
[
44
]
and
[
31
]
*Section 8, twisted local systems are used instead of local systems. Here we identify twisted local systems with local systems by choosing a basis of smooth 1-cycles together with a lifting in the circle bundle (by keeping track of their tangent vectors). This is equivalent to fixing a (relative) spin structure. See Appendix
B.2
and also
[
44
]
*Section 8.2 or
[
31
]
*Section 8.3.
Note that any 1-cycle in
H_{1}(\mathfrak{w};\mathbb{Z})
defines a regular function on
\mathcal{X}_{\mathfrak{w}}
by simply taking monodromy along the 1-cycle.

Proposition 6.18
.

Let
\mathfrak{w}\subset J^{1}\Sigma_{\text{op}}
be a free Legendrian weave with boundary being a Legendrian braid closure
\Lambda\subset J^{1}(\partial\Sigma_{\text{cl}})\subset T^{*,\infty}\Sigma_{\text{op}}
. Then there is an embedding of algebraic stacks

\mathcal{X}_{\mathfrak{w}}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{M}_{1}(\Sigma,\Lambda)_{0}.

Proof.

This follows from Theorem
5.5
and Subsection
5.3.3
. Indeed, the category of rank 1 local systems
\operatorname{Loc}^{1}(\mathfrak{w})
embeds into
\operatorname{Sh}^{1}_{\mathfrak{w}}(\Sigma\times\mathbb{R})
, and taking proper push forward via the projection defines a fully faithful embedding into
\operatorname{Sh}^{1}_{\Lambda}(\Sigma)
. Thus we know that

H^{1}(\mathfrak{w},\Bbbk^{\times})\hookrightarrow\mathcal{M}_{1}(\Sigma,\Lambda)_{0}

is an embedding of algebraic stacks.
∎

As a corollary, we obtain the non-abelianization map from sheaf quantization of Legendrian weaves. The word non-abelianization
[
20
]
refers to the process of getting a framed (non-abelian)
\operatorname{GL}_{n}
-local system on
\Sigma
from an (abelian)
\operatorname{GL}_{1}
-local system on
\mathfrak{w}
.

Corollary 6.19
.

Let
\Sigma
be a surface with marked points and
\mathfrak{w}_{n}^{*}\subset J^{1}\Sigma_{\text{op}}
be the free Legendrian weave associated to the ideal
n
-triangulation. Then there is a non-abelianization map

\mathcal{X}_{\mathfrak{w}_{n}^{*}}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{X}_{\operatorname{GL}_{n}}(\Sigma).

Proof.

This follows from Proposition
6.16
and Proposition
6.18
. Indeed, the condition that the
n
-graph
\mathbb{G}_{n}^{*}
has no vertices at the punctures ensures that
\mathfrak{w}_{n}^{*}
is a Legendrian weave with boundary
\bigcirc_{n}\subset J^{1}(\partial\Sigma_{\text{cl}})
being the trivial braid. Then the result follows from the fact that
\mathcal{M}_{1}(\Sigma,\bigcirc_{n})_{0}\cong\mathcal{X}_{\operatorname{GL}_{n}}(\Sigma)
.
∎

Remark 6.20
.

We can consider a basis of a subspace in
H_{1}(\mathfrak{w}_{n}^{*};\mathbb{Z})
(see Lemma
2.7
) given by the
\sf I,\sf Y
-cycles and the small loops around the marked points, forming the vertices of the quiver defining the cluster variety. The small loops around marked points are frozen variables (which cannot be mutated), and by forgetting the frozen variables one gets the cluster coordinates on
\mathcal{X}_{\operatorname{PGL}_{n}}(\Sigma)
via the projection map in Remark
6.15
(compare Remark
6.24
). Indeed, one can compare the basis of
H_{1}
for the Legendrian surface in Corollary
4.2
and
4.4
, where the difference is exactly the loops around the marked points.

Remark 6.21
.

If we forget the data of the flags near the punctures, this will produce a map from
\mathcal{X}_{\mathfrak{w}}
to the moduli space of rank
n
local systems on
\Sigma_{\text{cl}}
, or equivalently, the moduli space of microlocal rank
n
sheaves with singular support on the 1-strand braid
\bigcirc_{1}
around the punctures
\mathcal{M}_{n}(\Sigma,\bigcirc_{1})_{0}
. This is just the microlocal non-abelianization map introduced in
[
65
]
*Section 3.1 or the Legendrian degeneration in
[
70
]
*Section 7.

The cluster
\mathcal{X}
-variables
[
20
,
21
]
in the study of spectral networks are defined via the non-abelianization map, by the monodromies of the rank 1 local system in
\mathcal{X}_{\mathfrak{w}}
along a specific collection of 1-cycles. These 1-cycles are defined by three adjacent branched points of the spectral covering
[
21
]
*Section 5.9, Figure 8 & 9, and under the diffeomorphism between the spectral covers and (Lagrangian projections of) the weaves, are exactly the
\sf Y
-cycles in the weave defined by three adjacent blue trivalent vertices in Corollary
4.2
.

By Theorem
5.5
, the monodromies of the local systems in
\operatorname{Loc}^{1}(\mathfrak{w})
are the same as the microlocal monodromies of the sheaf quantization in
\operatorname{Sh}^{1}_{\mathfrak{w}}(\Sigma\times\mathbb{R})
. Therefore, Gaiotto-Moore-Neitzke’s cluster
\mathcal{X}
-variables are the same as the microlocal monodromies of the sheaf quantizations along
\sf Y
-cycles specified in Corollary
4.2
.

6.2.3.
Dimers and sheaves over conjugate Lagrangians

Dimer models in cluster varieties, based on bipartite graphs, are discussed in
[
15
,
30
]
and they are shown to yield cluster
\mathcal{X}
-variables on the moduli of framed
\operatorname{GL}_{n}
-local systems on punctured surfaces. In this context, we start with a Riemann surface
\Sigma
and a bipartite graph
\mathbb{G}\subset\Sigma
. Let
\mathcal{X}_{\mathbb{G}}=H^{1}(\mathbb{G};\Bbbk^{\times})
be the moduli space of rank 1 local systems on
\mathbb{G}
. Any null region
F
(meaning neither black nor white) in
\Sigma\backslash\mathbb{G}
, which are called faces in
[
30
]
, defines a cycle
\gamma_{F}\in H_{1}(\mathbb{G};\mathbb{Z})
. They can be viewed as regular functions on
\mathcal{X}_{\mathbb{G}}
. At this stage the following is rather immediate:

Proposition 6.22
.

Let
\mathbb{G}
be a bipartite graph on a surface
\Sigma
, and
\Lambda(\mathbb{G})
be the alternating Legendrian link determined by
\mathbb{G}
. Let
\mathcal{M}_{1}(\Sigma,\Lambda(\mathbb{G}))_{0}
the moduli space of microlocal rank 1 sheaves in
\operatorname{Sh}^{1}_{\Lambda(\mathbb{G})}(\Sigma)
with acyclic stalks at the marked points in
\Sigma
. Then there is an embedding of algebraic stacks

\mathcal{X}_{\mathbb{G}}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{M}_{1}(\Sigma,\Lambda(\mathbb{G}))_{0}.

Proof.

Note that the conjugate Lagrangian
L(\mathbb{G})
associated to
\Lambda(\mathbb{G})
admits a deformation retraction to the graph
\mathbb{G}
. Thus
\mathcal{X}_{\mathbb{G}}\cong H^{1}(L(\mathbb{G});\Bbbk^{\times})
, both equipped with the coordinate system given by monodromies along
\gamma_{F}
for null regions
F
. Following Theorem
5.5
, we get a fully faithful embedding of
\operatorname{Loc}^{1}(L(\mathbb{G}))
into
\operatorname{Sh}^{1}_{\Lambda(\mathbb{G})}(\Sigma)
, and hence an embedding of algebraic stacks

H^{1}(L(\mathbb{G});\Bbbk^{\times})\hookrightarrow\mathcal{M}_{1}(\Sigma,\Lambda(\mathbb{G}))_{0},

and this completes the proof.
∎

With the above preparations established, we now turn to the following specific setting in Corollary
1.3
. Following
[
30
]
, we can consider a bipartite graph
\mathbb{G}_{n}^{*}
that come from an ideal
n
-triangulation on a Riemann surface
\Sigma
. It is implicit in
[
30
]
, and explicitly written (in a more general setting) in
[
31
]
that the moduli space of rank 1 local systems on
\Gamma_{n}
embeds into the moduli space of rank
n
framed local systems. Now we can use the Hamiltonian isotopies from Theorem
1.1
so as to conclude the comparison between the bipartite graph dimer model and the spectral cover reads as follows:

Corollary 6.23
.

Let
\Sigma
be a closed surface with marked points
\{x_{1},...,x_{m}\}
and an associated ideal
n
-triangulation. Let
\mathbb{G}_{n}^{*}
be the
A_{n}^{*}
-bipartite graph associated to the ideal
n
-triangulation. Then there is an embedding of algebraic varieties

\mathcal{X}_{\mathbb{G}_{n}^{*}}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{X}_{\operatorname{GL}_{n}}(\Sigma).

In addition, for
\mathfrak{w}_{n}^{*}
the spectral cover of the
n
-triangulation, there is a canonical isomorphism
\mathcal{X}_{\mathbb{G}_{n}^{*}}\cong\mathcal{X}_{\mathfrak{w}_{n}^{*}}
such that the embedding is identified with the embedding

\mathcal{X}_{\mathfrak{w}_{n}^{*}}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{X}_{\operatorname{GL}_{n}}(\Sigma)

defined by sheaf quantization of Legendrian weaves in Corollary
6.19
.

Proof.

It follows immediately from Theorem
1.1
that the Hamiltonian isotopy from the conjugate Lagrangian
L(\mathbb{G}_{n}^{*})
to the projection
w_{n}^{*}
of the Legendrian weave
\mathfrak{w}_{n}^{*}
defines an isomorphism
H^{1}(\mathbb{G}_{n}^{*},\Bbbk^{\times})\cong H^{1}(\mathfrak{w}_{n}^{*},\Bbbk^{\times})
. Proposition
6.16
and Corollary
6.19
therefore implies the existence of the embedding

H^{1}(\mathbb{G}_{n}^{*},\Bbbk^{\times})\xrightarrow{\sim}H^{1}(\mathfrak{w}_{n}^{*},\Bbbk^{\times})\hookrightarrow\mathcal{X}_{\operatorname{GL}_{n}}(\Sigma).

By Hamiltonian invariance in Proposition
5.6
we know that this embedding coincides with the embedding in Proposition
6.22
.
∎

Remark 6.24
.

One can also consider a basis of
H_{1}(\mathbb{G}_{n}^{*};\mathbb{Z})
given by all the null regions of the bipartite graph, forming the vertices of the quiver defining the cluster variety. Then the null regions around marked points are frozen variables (which cannot be mutated), and by forgetting the frozen variables one gets the cluster coordinates on
\mathcal{X}_{\operatorname{PGL}_{n}}(\Sigma)
via the projection map in Remark
6.15
(compare Remark
6.20
). Indeed, one can compare the basis of
H_{1}
for the corresponding
A_{n}^{*}
-bipartite graphs as in Subsection
2.1.1
, or Corollary
4.2
and
4.4
, where the difference is exactly the null regions around the marked points.

Corollary
6.23
shows that in fact, the cluster coordinates
\mathcal{X}_{\mathbb{G}_{n}^{*}}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{X}_{\operatorname{GL}_{n}}(\Sigma)
defined by microlocal monodromies of sheaf quantizations of conjugate Lagrangians are identical as the cluster coordinates
\mathcal{X}_{\mathfrak{w}_{n}^{*}}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{X}_{\operatorname{GL}_{n}}(\Sigma)
defined by microlocal monodromies of sheaf quantizations of the Legendrian weaves.

6.2.4.
Proof of Theorem
1.3

Corollary
6.19
implies that the cluster coordinates in (3) & (4) are identical, and Corollary
6.23
implies that the cluster coordinates in (1) & (3) are identical. Therefore it suffices to identify the Fock-Goncharov cluster coordinates in (2) with any other class of cluster coordinates.

For that, note that Fock-Goncharov’s cluster
\mathcal{X}
-coordinates on the moduli of framed local systems
\mathcal{X}_{\operatorname{GL}_{n}}(\Sigma)

[
15
,
30
]
are exactly defined by an embedding
\mathcal{X}_{\mathbb{G}_{n}^{*}}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{X}_{\operatorname{GL}_{n}}(\Sigma)
. For each null component (or face) in the alternating coloring, they assigned a rational function, whose explicit formulas are the cross ratios and triple products as in Section
5.3.2
. The 1-cycles are in
H_{1}(\mathbb{G}_{n}^{*};\mathbb{Z})
but the functions are microlocal monodromies of sheaves over
H_{1}(\mathfrak{w}_{n}^{*};\mathbb{Z})
. By Corollary
4.2
, the isomorphism
\mathcal{X}_{\mathbb{G}_{n}^{*}}\cong\mathcal{X}_{\mathfrak{w}_{n}^{*}}
identifies standard monodromy functions along the standard 1-cycles on both sides. Therefore the microlocal monodromies along 1-cycles in the Legendrian weave are exactly the ones along the corresponding 1-cycles in the conjugate Lagrangian. Hence the Fock-Goncharov cluster coordinates in (2) are indeed the cluster coordinates defined by microlocal monodromies.

Appendix A
DG-Categories in the microlocal theory of sheaves

Let
M
be a smooth manifold,
\Lambda\subseteq T^{*,\infty}M
a real subanalytic Legendrian submanifold,
L\subseteq T^{*}M
an embedded exact Lagrangian such that
\partial_{\infty}L=\Lambda
and
\widetilde{L}\subseteq J^{1}M
its Legendrian lift. Let
\operatorname{mod}(\Bbbk)
be the dg-category of chain complexes of
\Bbbk
-modules,
\Bbbk
a field of characteristic zero. The following diagram displays dg-categories that are relevant in the study of the Legendrian isotopy class of
\Lambda
:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Loc}^{pp}(\Lambda)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Loc}^{cpt}(\Lambda)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Loc}(\Lambda)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Sh}^{pp}_{\Lambda}(M)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Sh}^{cpt}_{\Lambda}(M)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Sh}^{ctr}_{\Lambda}(M)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Sh}_{\Lambda}(M)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Sh}^{pp}_{\widetilde{L}}(M\times\overline{\mathbb{R}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Sh}^{cpt}_{\widetilde{L}}(M\times\overline{\mathbb{R}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Sh}^{ctr}_{\widetilde{L}}(M\times\overline{\mathbb{R}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Sh}_{\widetilde{L}}(M\times\overline{\mathbb{R}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Loc}^{pp}(\widetilde{L})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Loc}^{cpt}(\widetilde{L})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Loc}(\widetilde{L})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m_{\Lambda}^{L}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m_{\Lambda}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(i^{-1}_{M\times\{+\infty\}})^{L}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m_{\Lambda}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{\iota}_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i^{-1}_{M\times\{+\infty\}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m_{\widetilde{L}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{\iota}_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{\iota}_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i^{-1}_{M\times\{+\infty\}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m_{\widetilde{L}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i^{-1}_{M\times\{+\infty\}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m_{\widetilde{L}}^{L}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i^{-1}_{\Lambda}}

By definition, the chart legend for the above categories reads as follows:

(a)

\operatorname{Loc}(\Lambda)
is the dg-derived category of local systems of
\operatorname{mod}(\Bbbk)
on
\Lambda
, i.e. locally constant sheaves of
\Bbbk
-modules on
\Lambda
.

Let us assume that
\Lambda
is connected and
*_{\Lambda}\in\Lambda
is a base point. A computationally useful characterization of
\operatorname{Loc}(\Lambda)
is that it is dg-equivalent to the dg-category of dg-modules on the dg-algebra
C_{-*}(\Omega\Lambda,*_{\Lambda};\Bbbk)
of chains over the loop space, endowed with the Pontryagin product. Indeed, by
[
47
, Appendix A.4]
,
\operatorname{Loc}(\Lambda)
is equivalent to the category of (quasi)functors
\operatorname{Fun}_{Simp}(\mbox{Sing}(\Lambda,*_{\Lambda}),N_{dg}(\operatorname{mod}(\Bbbk)))
between simplicial sets, where
\mbox{Sing}(\Lambda)
is the Kan complex of singular simplices of
\Lambda
,
*_{\Lambda}
is a base point, and
N_{dg}
is the dg-nerve from the dg-category of dg-categories to simplicial sets. By adjunction, this is equivalent to the dg-category of dg-functors
\operatorname{Fun}_{dg}(N_{dg}^{L}(\mbox{Sing}(\Lambda,*_{\Lambda})),\operatorname{mod}(\Bbbk))
where
N_{dg}^{L}
is the left adjoint of
N_{dg}
. It is then computed that the dg-category
N_{dg}^{L}(\mbox{Sing}(\Lambda,*_{\Lambda}))
is dg-equivalent to the dg-category (with one object) associated to
C_{-*}(\Omega\Lambda,*_{\Lambda};\Bbbk)
; e.g. see
[
58
]
for details.

(b)

\operatorname{Loc}^{cpt}(\Lambda)
is the full dg-subcategory of compact objects in
\operatorname{Loc}(\Lambda)
, and
\operatorname{Loc}^{pp}(\Lambda)
is the full dg-subcategory of local systems in
\operatorname{Loc}(\Lambda)
whose stalks are perfect
\Bbbk
-modules.
Let
C_{-*}(\Omega\Lambda,*_{\Lambda};\Bbbk)
denote the algebra of chains on the based loop space, as above, and
\operatorname{mod}(C_{-*}(\Omega\Lambda,*_{\Lambda};\Bbbk))
its dg-category of dg-modules over
\Bbbk
. Then
\operatorname{Loc}(\Lambda)\cong\operatorname{mod}(C_{-*}(\Omega\Lambda,*_{\Lambda};\Bbbk))
and we have the two isomorphisms:

\hskip 30.0pt\operatorname{Loc}^{pp}(\Lambda)\cong\operatorname{Fun}^{ex}(C_{-*}(\Omega\Lambda,*_{\Lambda};\Bbbk),\operatorname{Perf}(\Bbbk)),\quad\operatorname{Loc}^{cpt}(\Lambda)\cong\operatorname{Perf}(C_{-*}(\Omega\Lambda,*_{\Lambda};\Bbbk)).

In the case of
\operatorname{Loc}^{cpt}
, the stalks of the local systems are perfect as
C_{-*}(\Omega\Lambda,*_{\Lambda};\Bbbk)
-modules (and thus typically infinite rank
\Bbbk
-modules), whereas local systems in
\operatorname{Loc}^{pp}
have a stalk that is a perfect
\Bbbk
-module.

(c)

\operatorname{Sh}_{\Lambda}(M)
is the dg-derived category of sheaves of
\operatorname{mod}(\Bbbk)
.

First, there is the need to at least consider sheaves of chain complexes, and not just
\Bbbk
-modules, for
\operatorname{Sh}_{\Lambda}(M)
to be invariant under Legendrian isotopies of
\Lambda
. In that sense, we are lead to consider (classically) derived categories and it is possible to work with the derived category of sheaves without enhancing to dg-categories. This is the approach in
[
41
,
32
]
. Nevertheless, since the invariance result
[
32
]
actually builds the equivalences through convolution with a sheaf kernel, it also implies that the dg-category
\operatorname{Sh}_{\Lambda}(M)
is a Legendrian isotopy invariant, see
[
60
]
. Two advantages of working with dg-categories are:

(i)

For a dg-category
\mathcal{C}
of finite type,
[
68
]
shows that there exists a
D^{-}
-stack locally geometric and locally of finite presentation which parametrizes pseudo-perfect objects in
\mathcal{C}
. This allows for the passage to commutative (homotopical) algebraic geometry
[
69
]
, often more manageable than the non-commutative setting of dg-categories.

(ii)

Mapping cones are functorial for dg-categories. Note that key constructions in symplectic topology use mapping cones (and cocones), e.g. S. Guillermou’s sheaf quantization
[
34
, Part 11–12]
.
32
32

32

Indeed, the quantization of the double uses the convolution
k_{\gamma}\star F
for the functor
\Psi
, as presented (and with the notation) in
[
34
, Part 11]
. The restriction of
\Bbbk_{\gamma}\star F
to
u=\varepsilon
is a sheaf isomorphism to the mapping cone of the sheaf
F
with an
\varepsilon
-Reeb push-off of
F
.
In addition,
\operatorname{Sh}_{\Lambda}(M)
is a sheaf (and a cosheaf) on
M
, whereas their homotopical categories, which are the (classically) derived categories, are only pre-stacks.

Second, more technically, we can consider the dg-categories of chain complexes of sheaves or sheaves of chain complexes. There is a natural functor from the former to the latter, by associating to the sheafification of the corresponding pre-sheaf of chain complexes to each complex of sheaves on
X
.
33
33

33

See the Mathoverflow thread “Sheaves of complexes and complexes of sheaves” and references therein.
This is an equivalence for a smooth manifold
M
of finite Lebesgue covering dimension, which is indeed the case for any manifolds in this manuscript. Finally, there is a choice of support for the chain complexes: typical choices are unbounded, bounded, non-negatively graded, or non-positively graded. We work in the unbounded setting, or the locally bounded one, following the results from
[
59
]
.

(d)

\operatorname{Sh}^{ctr}_{\Lambda}(M)
is the dg-derived category of constructible sheaves of
\operatorname{mod}(\Bbbk)
. This is a dg-subcategory of
\operatorname{Sh}^{ctr}_{\Lambda}(M)
and, in the cases that the Legendrian
\Lambda
is subanalytic, the inclusion yields an equivalence of dg-categories. Since all fronts for the Legendrian links
\Lambda
being considered in this article are subanalytic, and thus Whitney stratified, we indistinguishable work with
\operatorname{Sh}^{ctr}_{\Lambda}(M)
or
\operatorname{Sh}_{\Lambda}(M)
. The first statement in
[
50
, Lemma 3.11]
shows that
\operatorname{Sh}^{ctr}_{\Lambda}(M)
is compactly generated by the microlocal skyscrapers
[
50
, Definitinion 3.14]
. (It is not split generated by these objects though.)

(e)

\operatorname{Sh}^{cpt}_{\Lambda}(M)
is the full dg-subcategory of compact objects in
\operatorname{Sh}^{ctr}_{\Lambda}(M)
. This category is split generated by the microlocal skyscrapers and its ind-completion yields
\operatorname{Sh}^{ctr}_{\Lambda}(M)
. The category
\operatorname{Sh}^{cpt}_{\Lambda}(M)
is a cosheaf on
M
. It follows from
[
50
]
that
\operatorname{Sh}^{cpt}_{\Lambda}(M)
is a dg-category of finite-type, as it is a finite colimit of finite-type dg-categories. This is relevant so as to apply
[
68
]
to conclude that its moduli of pseudo-perfect objects is a locally geometric derived stack (and locally of finite presentation). In the examples of this manuscript, the moduli we consider are even Artin stacks.

Note that
[
50
]
refers to
\operatorname{Sh}^{cpt}_{\Lambda}(M)
as
wrapped
sheaves. We have opted for
\operatorname{Sh}^{cpt}_{\Lambda}(M)
so as to emphasize that wrapping is actually related to the singular support condition
\operatorname{Sh}_{\Lambda}(M)
and not the subcategory
\operatorname{Sh}^{cpt}_{\Lambda}(M)
itself. That is, the sheaves in
\operatorname{Sh}_{\Lambda}(M)
are already geometrically
wrapped
in that geometric wrapping (partially stopped to
\Lambda
) truly occurs when taking the left adjoint to the inclusion
\operatorname{Sh}_{\Lambda}(M)
into
\operatorname{Sh}(M)
, see
[
43
, Theorem 1.2]
.

(f)

\operatorname{Sh}^{pp}_{\Lambda}(M)
is the full dg-subcategory of sheaves in
\operatorname{Sh}_{\Lambda}(M)
whose stalks are perfect
\Bbbk
-modules.
The category
\operatorname{Sh}^{cpt}_{\Lambda}(M)
is a sheaf on
M
. It is proven in
[
50
, Theorem 3.21]
or
[
24
, Corollary 4.22]
that the hom-pairing provides an equivalence between
\operatorname{Sh}^{pp}_{\Lambda}(M)
and the dg-category of exact functors
\operatorname{Fun}^{ex}(\operatorname{Sh}^{cpt}_{\Lambda}(M),\operatorname{Perf}(\Bbbk))
, where
\operatorname{Perf}(\Bbbk)
is the dg-category of perfect
\Bbbk
-modules. In that sense, objects in
\operatorname{Sh}^{pp}_{\Lambda}(M)
can be referred to as the pseudo-perfect (equiv. proper) objects in
\operatorname{Sh}^{cpt}_{\Lambda}(M)
, thence the notation.

Finally, the functors
\iota_{2},\iota_{1}
and
\iota_{0}
are the defining inclusions. The fact that

\operatorname{Loc}^{pp}(\Lambda)\hookrightarrow\operatorname{Loc}^{cpt}(\Lambda),\;\text{and}\;\operatorname{Sh}^{pp}_{\Lambda}(M)\hookrightarrow\operatorname{Sh}^{cpt}_{\Lambda}(M)

uses the result that
\operatorname{Loc}^{cpt}(\Lambda)
and
\operatorname{Sh}^{cpt}_{\Lambda}(M)
are smooth categories
[
25
, Corollary 4.25 & Lemma A.8]
. The functor
i_{M\times\{+\infty\}}^{-1}
is given by the restriction from
M\times\overline{\mathbb{R}}
(with the stratification determined by
\widetilde{L}
) to its boundary
M\times\{+\infty\}
(with the stratification determined by
\Lambda
). The functor
m_{\Lambda}
is the microlocal functor discussed in Appendix
B
. Note that the functor

m_{\Lambda}
is defined to map to the global sections
\mu\operatorname{sh}_{\Lambda}(\Lambda)
of the Kashiwara-Schapira stack. In general, there can be obstructions to identify
\mu\operatorname{sh}_{\Lambda}(\Lambda)
with
\operatorname{Loc}(\Lambda)
, and even then there is a subtlety with regards to
twisted
local systems. For the Legendrian links in this manuscript, these obstructions vanish and twisting is addressed in Appendix
B.2
.

Neither of the two functors
i_{M\times\{+\infty\}}^{-1}
or
m_{\Lambda}
preserve compact objects. Nevertheless, each of them admits a left adjoint, e.g. see
[
25
, Lemma 4.12]
and references therein. These left adjoints
(i_{M\times\{+\infty\}}^{-1})^{L}
and
m_{\Lambda}^{L}
preserve compact objects and thus given rise to functors among
\operatorname{Sh}^{cpt}_{\Lambda}(M)
,
\operatorname{Sh}^{cpt}_{\widetilde{L}}(M\times\overline{\mathbb{R}})
and
\operatorname{Loc}^{cpt}(\Lambda)
in the opposite direction.

In this manuscript we focus on
\Lambda
as a Legendrian submanifold of the ideal contact boundary
T^{*,\infty}M
. That said, from the perspective of Lagrangian skeleta, it is natural to instead use the (singular) Lagrangian given by the union of
M
and the Lagrangian cone
\mbox{cone}(\Lambda)
of
\Lambda
. The global sections of the Kashiwara-Schapira stack on
M\cup\mbox{cone}(\Lambda)
lead to
\operatorname{Sh}_{\Lambda}(M)
and thus the categories above appear naturally as well. The perspective of Lagrangian skeleta and (co)sheaves of categories on them are also useful in the study of Weinstein manifolds.

Appendix B
Results on the microlocal theory of constructible sheaves

The goal of this appendix is to review and modify the necessary results in the microlocal theory of sheaves, as it relates to studying Lagrangian fillings, and fill in the gaps and necessary comparisons between the works in the current literature. The appendix consists of three separate parts, as follows.

(1)

Firstly, in Section
B.2
we review S. Guillermou’s result on microlocalization
[
33
]
, discuss the role of twisted local systems, and provide a combinatoric approach for the computation with coherent signs refining the construction in
[
66
]
, which is not systematically written in literature.

(2)

Secondly, in Section
B.3
we present Jin-Treumann’s result on sheaf quantization
[
39
]
and make modifications on their original results, which is not compatible with the current constructible sheaf model on Legendrian weaves. In particular, we prove Hamiltonian invariance property without fixing boundary conditions as this is needed for the applications in our setting; this modification was also not written in literature.

(3)

Thirdly, in Section
B.4
, we define moduli and framed moduli of sheaves with singular support on the Legendrians, compare different approaches to framed moduli of sheaves, and define microlocal merodromies along relative 1-cycles.

B.1.
Singular support of sheaves

For sheaves on manifolds, M. Kashiwara and P. Schapira introduced in
[
41
]
the concept of singular support of (complexes of) sheaves, which detect when derived sections fail to extend along a certain codirection; they refer to it as the derived sections fail to propagate. Let
\operatorname{Sh}(M)
be the dg-derived category of sheaves on
M
in the sense of
[
42
]
*Section 4.4, i.e. dg category of complexes of sheaves with unbounded cohomology localized along quasi-isomorphisms, see Appendix
A
. Following
[
66
]
*Section 3, Example
5.3
gave basic examples of what sheaves with a Legendrian singular support are like. The general definition reads as follows.

Definition B.1

(
[
41
]
)
.

For a complex of sheaves
\mathcal{F}\in\operatorname{Sh}(M)
, the singular support
SS(\mathcal{F})\subset T^{*}M
is the closure of points
(x,\xi)
such that for some neighbourhood
U_{x}
of
x
,
\varphi\in C^{\infty}(M)
,
d\varphi(x)=\xi
,

\mathrm{Cone}\left(\Gamma(U_{x},\mathcal{F})\rightarrow\Gamma(U_{x}\cap\varphi^{-1}((-\infty,0)),\mathcal{F})\right)\not\simeq 0.

SS^{\infty}(\mathcal{F})=\overline{SS(\mathcal{F})}\cap T^{*,\infty}M
.

In
[
41
, Theorem 6.5.4]
it is shown that
SS^{\infty}(\mathcal{F})
is a (singular) coisotropic subset. By
[
41
, Theorem 8.4.2]
, if
SS^{\infty}(\mathcal{F})
is a (singular) Legendrian, then
\mathcal{F}
is a constructible sheaf. Following Appendix
A
, given a (subanalytic) Legendrian
\Lambda\subset T^{*,\infty}M
,
\operatorname{Sh}_{\Lambda}(M)
denotes the full dg-subcategory in
\operatorname{Sh}(M)
of constructible sheaves
\mathcal{F}
such that
SS^{\infty}(\mathcal{F})\subset\Lambda
.

B.2.
Microlocalization functor

Given a constructible sheaf
\mathcal{F}\in\operatorname{Sh}_{\Lambda}(M)
, there is a microlocalization functor
m_{\Lambda}
which produces a (twisted) local system on the Legendrian
\Lambda\subset T^{*,\infty}M
. Microlocalization has been studied in
[
41
]
*Chapter 6,
[
33
]
*Section 6–11 (or
[
34
]
*Part 10), and also
[
50
,
39
]
. We summarize their results and in particular give a combinatorial approach of computing microlocal monodromies with coherent signs, refining the combinatorial construction in
[
66
]
*Section 5.

B.2.1.
Microlocalization with twisted coefficients

In
[
66
]
, a microlocal monodromy map for a sheaf
\mathcal{F}\in\operatorname{Sh}_{\Lambda}(M)
is constructed in a combinatorial way, as in Example
5.4
. Their construction fits into a more general framework of microlocalization, see
[
41
, Chapter IV]
. In general, we can consider a sheaf of categories
\mu\operatorname{Sh}_{\Lambda}
on
T^{*,\infty}M
, the Kashiwara-Schapira stack
[
33
,
34
]
or the brane category
[
39
]
, as follows. Here, working with dg-categories, we adopt the definitions in
[
49
, Section 5]
.

Definition B.2
.

Let
\Lambda\subset T^{*,\infty}M
. The presheaf of categories
\mu\operatorname{Sh}_{\Lambda}^{\text{pre}}
on
T^{*,\infty}M
associated to
\Lambda
is defined by, for any
\Omega\subset T^{*,\infty}M
,

\mu\operatorname{Sh}_{\Lambda}^{\text{pre}}(\Omega):=\operatorname{Sh}_{\Lambda\cup(T^{*,\infty}M\backslash\Omega)}(M)/\operatorname{Sh}_{T^{*,\infty}M\backslash\Omega}(M).

By definition,
\mu\operatorname{Sh}_{\Lambda}
is the sheafification of
\mu\operatorname{Sh}_{\Lambda}^{\text{pre}}
, which is induced by a sheaf of categories on
\Lambda
.

Finally, by definition, the microlocalization functor

m_{\Lambda}:\operatorname{Sh}_{\Lambda}(M)\rightarrow\mu\operatorname{Sh}_{\Lambda}(\Lambda)

is the natural quotient functor on the sheaf of categories.

Remark B.3
.

It can be verified that the definition above is the same as
[
33
, Section 5]
or
[
34
, Part 10]
, using
[
41
, Proposition 6.1.2]
, which proves that the stalks of the two sheaves of categories coincide. Alternatively, one can follow the definition in
[
50
, Section 3.4]
, that for a carefully chosen pair of small open balls
B\subset M
,
\Omega\subset T^{*,\infty}M
such that
\pi(\Omega)\subset B
and
\Omega
is a neighbourhood of a component of
\Lambda\cap T^{*,\infty}B
,

\mu\operatorname{Sh}_{\Lambda}(\Omega)=\operatorname{Sh}_{\Lambda\cup(T^{*,\infty}B\backslash\Omega)}(B).

The equivalence of these two definitions relies on existence of adjoints to the inclusion
\operatorname{Sh}_{\Lambda\cup(T^{*,\infty}B\backslash\Omega)}(B)

\hookrightarrow\operatorname{Sh}_{\Lambda\cup(T^{*,\infty}M\backslash\Omega)}(M)
. Similarly, in
[
39
, Section 3.8–11]
, the definition

\mu\operatorname{Sh}_{\Lambda}(\Omega)=\operatorname{Sh}_{\Lambda}(B)/\operatorname{Loc}(B)

is used. Equivalence between this definition and the above ones is an application of the refined microlocal cut-off lemma
[
33
, Lemma 6.7]
, see also
[
39
, Lemma 3.8]
.

Note that the microlocalization functor
m_{\Lambda}
restricts to
\operatorname{Sh}^{pp}_{\Lambda}(M)\rightarrow\mu\operatorname{Sh}_{\Lambda}^{pp}(\Lambda)
. In addition, the left adjoint
m_{\Lambda}^{L}
of microlocalization can be restricted to
\mu\operatorname{Sh}_{\Lambda}^{cpt}(\Lambda)\rightarrow\operatorname{Sh}^{cpt}_{\Lambda}(M)
; see Appendix
A
.

Let
M
and
\Lambda\subset T^{*,\infty}M
be oriented, and
\pi_{\Lambda\times\mathbb{R}_{+}}:\Lambda\times\mathbb{R}_{+}\rightarrow M
the projection where
\Lambda\times\mathbb{R}_{+}\subset T^{*}M\backslash M
is a conical Lagrangian. In our context, we have vanishing of Maslov classes. The (stabilized) oriented relative frame bundle over
\Lambda
is the principal
GL_{n,+}
-bundle defined by

\mathcal{I}_{\Lambda\times\mathbb{R}_{+}}:=\mathrm{Iso}_{+}(\pi_{\Lambda\times\mathbb{R}_{+}}^{*}TM,T(\Lambda\times\mathbb{R}_{+})).

Note that
\pi_{\Lambda}^{*}TM
splits as the direct sum of a Lagrangian subbundle in
\xi\subset T(T^{*,\infty}M)
and a rank 1 trivial line bundle tangent to the radius
\mathbb{R}_{+}
-direction in
TM
. Therefore, there is indeed a stabilization map

\mathcal{I}_{\Lambda}=\mathrm{Iso}_{+}(\pi_{\Lambda}^{*}((TM\backslash M)/\mathbb{R}_{+}),T\Lambda)\hookrightarrow\mathrm{Iso}_{+}(\pi_{\Lambda}^{*}TM,T(\Lambda\times\mathbb{R}_{+}))=\mathcal{I}_{\Lambda\times\mathbb{R}_{+}}.

Theorem B.4

(
[
33
]
*Theorem 11.5)
.

Let
M
be an oriented smooth manifold,
\Lambda\subset T^{*,\infty}M
be an oriented Legendrian with vanishing Maslov class, and
\mathcal{I}_{\Lambda\times\mathbb{R}_{+}}
be the oriented relative frame bundle over
\Lambda
. Then

\mu\operatorname{Sh}_{\Lambda}(\Lambda)\simeq\operatorname{Loc}_{\epsilon}(\mathcal{I}_{\Lambda\times\mathbb{R}_{+}})

where
\operatorname{Loc}_{\epsilon}(\mathcal{I}_{\Lambda\times\mathbb{R}_{+}})
is the dg-derived category of twisted local systems on
\mathcal{I}_{\Lambda\times\mathbb{R}_{+}}
, i.e. local systems whose monodromy along each fiber is
-1
.

Remark B.5
.

Twisting appears as follows, see also
[
33
]
*Section 9 & 10.2 or
[
34
]
*Section 10.3 & 10.6. For simplicity, assume that
\pi(\Lambda)
is defined a smooth hypersurface in a neighbourhood of
\pi(p)
. Suppose
\pi(\Lambda)
is defined by a function
\phi_{\Lambda}
where

\phi_{\Lambda}^{-1}(0)=\pi(\Lambda),\;\;d\phi_{\Lambda}(\pi(\Lambda))\subset\Lambda\times\mathbb{R}_{+}.

In computing the microstalk of a sheaf
\mathcal{F}\in Sh^{b}_{\Lambda}(M)
at
p\in\Lambda
, in general we do not simply consider the cone

\mathrm{Cone}(\Gamma(\phi_{\Lambda}^{-1}((-\infty,0],\mathcal{F})\rightarrow\Gamma(\phi_{\Lambda}^{-1}((-\infty,0)),\mathcal{F})).

In general, rather, we need to consider a function
\varphi
such that

\varphi^{-1}(0)\cap\pi(\Lambda)=\pi(p),\;\;d\varphi(\varphi^{-1}(0))\pitchfork\Lambda\times\mathbb{R}_{+},

and compute the local Morse group

\mathrm{Cone}(\Gamma(\varphi^{-1}((-\infty,0],\mathcal{F})\rightarrow\Gamma(\varphi^{-1}((-\infty,0)),\mathcal{F})),

which depends on the relative Morse index of
\phi_{\Lambda}
and
\varphi
at
\pi(p)
. There are different choices of Lagrangian planes
T_{p}\Lambda_{\varphi}
for
\Lambda_{\varphi}=\{(x,d\varphi(x))|x\in M\}
at a point
p\in\Lambda
, and any
\varphi
such that
\Lambda_{\varphi}\pitchfork\Lambda\times\mathbb{R}_{+}
determines a linear isomorphism

T_{p}(T_{\pi(p)}^{*}M)\rightarrow T_{p}\Lambda_{\varphi}\oplus T_{p}(\Lambda\times\mathbb{R}_{+})\rightarrow T_{p}(\Lambda\times\mathbb{R}_{+}).

The computation of
[
66
]
, where they essentially used the defining function
\phi_{\Lambda}
for the Legendrian, is equivalent to the choice of a function
\varphi
with Morse index 0 relative to
\pi(\Lambda)
plus the degree shifting by the Maslov potential.

Under the unique surjective homomorphism
\pi_{1}(GL_{n,+}(\mathbb{R}))\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}
,
n\geq 2
, the obstruction to the existence of a rank 1 twisted local system is characterized by a class in
H^{2}(\Lambda;\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z})
, which turns out to be the relative second Stiefel-Whitney class, see
[
34
]
.

Corollary B.6

(
[
33
]
*Theorem 11.5)
.

Let
\Lambda\subset T^{*,\infty}M
be oriented with vanishing Maslov class. If
\Lambda
is relatively spin, then there exists a rank 1 twisted local system on
\mathcal{I}_{\Lambda\times\mathbb{R}_{+}}
, and each choice of a rank 1 twisted local system determines an equivalence

\mu\operatorname{Sh}_{\Lambda}(\Lambda)\simeq\operatorname{Loc}_{\epsilon}(\mathcal{I}_{\Lambda\times\mathbb{R}_{+}})\simeq\operatorname{Loc}(\Lambda).

In particular, this recovers the combinatorial construction of

m_{\Lambda}:\operatorname{Sh}_{\Lambda}(M)\rightarrow\operatorname{Loc}(\Lambda)

in
[
65
]
up to signs. However, even though
\operatorname{Loc}_{\epsilon}(\mathcal{I}_{\Lambda})
and
\operatorname{Loc}(\Lambda)
are equivalent, we will still frequently use the twisted version
\operatorname{Loc}_{\epsilon}(\mathcal{I}_{\Lambda})
in the following sections, since the twisted version will give the correct signs that one will expect in cluster theory.
34
34

34

[
31
]
*Section 8.3, for example, explains the reason to consider twisted local systems in cluster theory.
[
20
,
44
]
also use twisted local systems.

Remark B.7
.

The stabilization process in Theorem
B.4
is important. For Legendrian knots, the oriented frame bundle is a
GL_{1,+}(\mathbb{R})
-bundle over
S^{1}
, hence
\mathcal{I}_{\Lambda}\simeq\Lambda
and there is no information on twisting in
\mathcal{I}_{\Lambda}
. However, the twisting data appear in the stabilized bundle
\mathcal{I}_{\Lambda\times\mathbb{R}_{+}}\simeq S^{1}\times\Lambda
. In fact, the sheaf categories are going to be stabilized in the sense that the classifying map of the Lagrangian factors through the stable Gauss map so that we obtain a stable
\infty
-category over ring spectra
[
39
]
, and hence we indeed need to study the Kashiwara-Schapira stack or twisted local systems up to stabilization induced by the embedding
\mathcal{I}_{\Lambda}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{I}_{\Lambda\times\mathbb{R}}\hookrightarrow\dots\hookrightarrow\mathcal{I}_{\Lambda\times\mathbb{R}^{N}}\hookrightarrow\dots
, see
[
33
]
*Section 10.1 or
[
34
]
*Section 10.6, which yields

\cdots\rightarrow\operatorname{Loc}_{\epsilon}(\mathcal{I}_{\Lambda\times\mathbb{R}^{N}})\rightarrow\cdots\rightarrow\operatorname{Loc}_{\epsilon}(\mathcal{I}_{\Lambda\times\mathbb{R}})\rightarrow\operatorname{Loc}_{\epsilon}(\mathcal{I}_{\Lambda}).

For example, when
\Lambda
arises as the boundary of a surface
\widetilde{L}
. Then the stabilization embedding provides a natural restriction map

\operatorname{Loc}_{\epsilon}(\mathcal{I}_{\widetilde{L}})\rightarrow\operatorname{Loc}_{\epsilon}(\mathcal{I}_{\Lambda\times\mathbb{R}})\rightarrow\operatorname{Loc}_{\epsilon}(\mathcal{I}_{\Lambda}).

B.2.2.
Microlocalization with coherent signs

Given a Legendrian
\Lambda\subset T^{*,\infty}M
that has vanishing Maslov class and vanishing relative second Stiefel-Whitney class, Corollary
B.6
shows a non-canonical equivalence

\mu\operatorname{Sh}_{\Lambda}(\Lambda)\simeq\operatorname{Loc}_{\epsilon}(\mathcal{I}_{\Lambda\times\mathbb{R}})\simeq\operatorname{Loc}(\Lambda).

Different choices of rank
1
twisted local systems may result in different identifications with the category of local systems. Here we discuss these choice, see also
[
33
]
*Section 9 & 10.2 or
[
34
]
*Section 10.3 & 10.6.

Lemma B.8
.

Let
\Lambda\subset T^{*,\infty}M
be relatively spin. Then there is a bijection between

(1)

relative spin structures on
\Lambda
,

(2)

rank 1 twisted local systems on
\operatorname{Loc}_{\epsilon}(\mathcal{I}_{\Lambda\times\mathbb{R}})
,

(3)

sections of the stable relative oriented frame bundle
\mathcal{I}_{\Lambda\times\mathbb{R}^{N}}
over the 2-skeleton
\Lambda_{\leq 2}
.

Proof.

For (1) & (3), since
\Lambda\subset T^{*,\infty}M
is relative spin, the relative oriented frame bundle

\mathcal{I}_{\Lambda\times\mathbb{R}}=\mathrm{Iso}_{+}(\pi_{\Lambda\times\mathbb{R}_{+}}^{*}TM,T(\Lambda\times\mathbb{R}_{+}))

is trivial over the 2-skeleton
\Lambda_{\leq 2}
, and any global section uniquely determines a relative spin structure on
\Lambda
. On the 1-skeleton
\Lambda_{\leq 1}\subset T^{*,\infty}\Sigma
, the section determines a trivialization where vanishing of the obstruction class in
H^{2}(\Lambda;\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z})
implies existence of an extension of the trivialization to the 2-skeleton. Since

\pi_{1}(\operatorname{GL}_{N,+}(\mathbb{R}))\xrightarrow{\sim}\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z},

the choice of a section on the 2-skeleton is classified by
H^{1}(\Lambda;\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z})
.

For (2) & (3), rank 1 (twisted) local system can be uniquely extended from a rank 1 (twisted) local system on the 2-skeleton
\Lambda_{\leq 2}
. Fix a rank 1 twisted local system
\mathcal{L}_{0}
on
\Lambda_{\leq 2}
. Then any rank 1 twisted local system can be obtained by
\mathcal{L}=\pi^{-1}s^{-1}\mathcal{L}_{0}
for a section
s
on the 2-skeleton
\Lambda_{\leq 2}
, and this correspondence is bijective.
∎

Remark B.9
.

When we choose a section on the 2-skeleton
\Lambda_{\leq 2}
, we are still doing computations on twisted local systems
\operatorname{Loc}_{\epsilon}(\mathcal{I}_{\Lambda\times\mathbb{R}})
, even though the choice of the section determines a relative spin structure and an identification with
\operatorname{Loc}(\Lambda)
.

Therefore, in order to compute the microlocal monodromy with signs, we will fix a relative spin structure and consider a section over the 2-skeleton
s:\Lambda_{\leq 2}\rightarrow\mathcal{I}_{\Lambda\times\mathbb{R}^{N}}|_{\Lambda_{\leq 2}}
.

Definition B.10
.

Let
\Lambda\subset T^{*,\infty}M
be a Legendrian that has vanishing Maslov class with a fixed relatively spin structure, determined by
s:\Lambda_{\leq 2}\rightarrow\mathcal{I}_{\Lambda\times\mathbb{R}^{N}}|_{\Lambda_{\leq 2}}
. Given a sheaf
\mathcal{F}\in\operatorname{Sh}_{\Lambda}(M)
, the (signed) microlocal monodromy along a path
\gamma\in H_{1}(\Lambda;\mathbb{Z})
is the monodromy along the lifting
s\circ\gamma\in H_{1}(\mathcal{I}_{\Lambda\times\mathbb{R}^{N}};\mathbb{Z})

m_{\Lambda,\gamma}(\mathcal{F})=m_{\Lambda}(\mathcal{F})_{s\circ\gamma}.

For a Legendrian knot, there are only 2 relative spin structures since
H^{1}(\Lambda;\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z})=\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}
. For the generator
\gamma\in H_{1}(\Lambda;\mathbb{Z})
, the relative spin structure only depends on the parity of the winding number
s\circ\gamma
along the fiber
\operatorname{GL}_{2,+}(\mathbb{R})
in
\mathcal{I}_{\Lambda\times\mathbb{R}}
. Note that under the stabilization map

\pi_{1}(\operatorname{GL}_{2,+}(\mathbb{R}))\rightarrow\pi_{1}(\operatorname{GL}_{N,+}(\mathbb{R}))\xrightarrow{\sim}\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}\xrightarrow{\sim}\mathbb{Z}^{\times},

the microlocal monodromy only depends on the parity of the winding number.

We now explain how the combinatorial microlocal monodromy of
[
66
]
*Section 5 can be enhanced to involve the choice of a lifting of a path in the relative oriented frame bundle.

Example B.11
.

For a Legendrian knot with no cusps in the front, when computing the microlocal monodromy at
(x,y,\xi,\eta)\in\Lambda
, following Remark
B.5
, the approach in
[
65
]
is equivalent to choosing the Lagrangian planes defined by
T_{p}\Lambda_{\varphi}
where

\Lambda_{\varphi}=\{(x,y,d\varphi(x,y))|(x,y)\in\mathbb{R}^{2}\},\;\;\varphi(x,y)=0,\,d\varphi(x,y)=(\xi,\eta)

such that
\varphi^{-1}(0)
has relative Morse index 0 relative to
\pi(\Lambda)
. One may observe that the winding number of this family of Lagrangian planes coincides with the winding number tangent vector in the fiber of
T\Sigma
(mod 2).

For a Legendrian
n
-satellite
\Lambda\subset T^{*,\infty}\mathbb{R}^{2}
of the outward unit conormal bundle of a disk, this family of Lagrangian planes determines a section in
\mathcal{I}_{\Lambda\times\mathbb{R}}
with winding number
n
(mod 2) around the fiber and thus corresponds to the Lie group spin structure when
n
is even and to the null-cobordant spin structure when
n
is odd.

Still for a Legendrian knot, we can also choose different spin structures on
S^{1}
when computing microlocal monodromies. In fact, such a choice can be localized near the set of points
P=\{p_{1},\dots,p_{n}\}
.

Definition B.12
.

For a Legendrian knot
\Lambda\subset T^{*,\infty}\Sigma
, a set of sign points is a discrete set of points
P
, such that for the generator
\gamma\in H_{1}(\Lambda;\mathbb{Z})
, the lifting
s\circ\gamma
winds around the fiber of
\mathcal{I}_{\Lambda\times\mathbb{R}^{N}}
once in a small neighbourhood of each sign point and follows the tangent vector of
\pi(\gamma)
away from sign points.

Example B.13
.

For a Legendrian knot, and a set of sign points
P=\{p_{1},\dots,p_{n}\}
, we can modify the sign in the computation of
[
66
]
by the number of sign points that we choose.

For a Legendrian
n
-satellite
\Lambda\subset T^{*,\infty}\mathbb{R}^{2}
of the outward unit conormal bundle of a disk, we may add no sign points when
n
is odd and add 1 sign point
p
when
n
is even, then the section in
\mathcal{I}_{\Lambda\times\mathbb{R}}
determined by the small loop around the fiber in a neighbourhood of
p
together with the family of Lagrangian planes of
[
66
]
will define the null-cobordant spin structure.

For an oriented Legendrian surface, relative spin structures are classified by
H^{1}(\Lambda;\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z})
. For a basis
\gamma_{i}\in H_{1}(\Lambda;\mathbb{Z})
, the relative spin structure depends on the parity of the winding number
s\circ\gamma_{i}
along the fiber
\operatorname{GL}_{3,+}(\mathbb{R})
in
\mathcal{I}_{\Lambda\times\mathbb{R}}
. Therefore, when fixing a relative spin structure, we in fact fix the liftings of a basis
\gamma_{i}\in H_{1}(\Lambda;\mathbb{Z})
.

The following definition is first made in
[
9
]
*Section 4.5 for Legendrian weaves, and we are simply borrowing the terminology from their work.

Definition B.14
.

For a closed Legendrian surface
\widetilde{L}\subset T^{*,\infty}(\Sigma\times\mathbb{R})
, a set of sign curves is a graph
P
whose vertices are the centers of 2-cells, such that for each generator
\gamma\in H_{1}(\widetilde{L};\mathbb{Z})
, the lifting
s\circ\gamma
winds around the fiber of
\mathcal{I}_{\Lambda\times\mathbb{R}^{N}}
once in neighbourhoods of intersections with sign curves, and follows the tangent vector of
\pi(\gamma)
away from sign points.

For a Legendrian surface
\widetilde{L}\subset T^{*,\infty}(\Sigma\times\mathbb{R})
with boundary on a Legendrian knot
\Lambda\subset T^{*,\infty}(\Sigma\times\{+\infty\})
(Section
2.3
), a set of sign curves is correspondingly a graph
P
whose vertices are the centers of 2-cells and 1-cells in the boundary.

Finally, a compatible collection of sign curves on
\widetilde{L}\subset T^{*,\infty}(\Sigma\times\mathbb{R})
is a a graph
P
whose vertices are the centers of 2-cells such that for every loop
\gamma=0\in H_{1}(\widetilde{L};\mathbb{Z})
that bounds a 2-cell, the winding number of the lifting
s\circ\gamma
around the fiber is 1.

The collections of sign curves in Definition
B.14
indeed exist:

Lemma B.15
.

For an oriented Legendrian surface
\widetilde{L}\subset T^{*,\infty}(\Sigma\times\mathbb{R})
, there exists a compatible collection of sign curves.

Proof.

We consider all the 2-cells attached inductively, which determines an (partial) order on all the 1-cells. For each loop 2-cell in
\Lambda
, we can compute the winding number of the tangent vector in the fiber of
T\Sigma
. Whenever the winding number is
0
(mod
2
), we add a sign point in any of the 1-cells contained in its boundary. Then connect all the sign points on
\gamma
with the centers of the adjacent 2-cells.

When
\Lambda
is a surface with boundary, we thus define a compatible collection of sign curves. When
\Lambda
is a closed surface, we can defines a compatible collection of sign curves on the compliment of a 2-cell
\Lambda\backslash D
. On the boundary of the 2-cell
D
, the spin structure determined by sign points is null-cobordant. Hence the parity of sign points determined by
\Lambda\backslash D
agrees with the parity determined by
D
. Therefore there is a compatible choice of sign curves.
∎

Example B.16
.

For the Legendrian unknot
\Lambda\subset T^{*,\infty}(S^{1}\times\mathbb{R})
which is the 2-satallite of the unknot with a Legendrian weave filling
\widetilde{L}\subset T^{*,\infty}(\mathbb{D}^{2}\times\mathbb{R})
, we can define the sign curve to be the curve from the center of the disk to a boundary sign point as in
[
9
]
*Example 4.28.

More generally, for a Legendrian weave
\widetilde{L}\subset T^{*,\infty}(\Sigma\times\mathbb{R})
, we can consider a triangulation on
\Sigma
such that each 2-cell contains 0 or 1 trivalent vertex (in the interior). This lifts to a triangulation of
\widetilde{L}
such that the parity of number of sign points on the boundary of each 2-cell agrees with the number of trivalent vertices inside (either 0 or 1). Therefore, we can choose sign curves to be a set of curves connecting trivalent vertices and
\partial\widetilde{L}
such that all trivalent vertices have degree 1. This is exactly the notion of a coherent collection of sign curves in
[
9
]
*Definition 4.15.

B.3.
Sheaf quantization results

We summarize the necessary results on sheaf quantizations in symplectic topology and contact topology.

B.3.1.
Sheaf quantization of Hamiltonian isotopy

The following theorem shows that the category
\operatorname{Sh}_{\Lambda}(M)
is an invariant of the Legendrian submanifold, which enables one to study the properties of a Legendrian by studying the constructible sheaf category. First, recall that for
\{\Lambda_{t}\}_{t\in[0,1]}
a family of Legendrians in
T^{*,\infty}M
induced by the contact Hamiltonian
H
, the Legendrian movie is

\Lambda_{H}=\{(x,\xi,t,\tau)|(x,\xi)=\varphi_{H}^{t}(x_{0},\xi_{0}),\tau=-H(x_{0},\xi_{0}),(x_{0},\xi_{0})\in\Lambda_{0}\},

where
\varphi_{H}^{t}\,(t\in[0,1])
is the contact flow induced by
H
such that
\Lambda_{t}=\varphi_{H}^{t}(\Lambda_{0})
.

Theorem B.17

(
[
32
]
)
.

Let
\{\Lambda_{t}\}_{t\in[0,1]}
be a Legendrian isotopy in
T^{*,\infty}M
induced by the contact Hamiltonian
H
. Then there is an equivalence of categories given by convolution of sheaf kernels

\Phi_{H}:\,\operatorname{Sh}_{\Lambda_{0}}(M)\xleftarrow{i_{0}^{-1}}\operatorname{Sh}_{\Lambda_{H}}(M\times[0,1])\xrightarrow{i_{1}^{-1}}\operatorname{Sh}_{\Lambda_{1}}(M),

where
i_{t}:M\times\{t\}\hookrightarrow M\times[0,1]
are the inclusions. In addition, the equivalence preserves microlocal rank
r
objects on both sides.

Remark B.18
.

The equivalence
\operatorname{Sh}_{\Lambda_{0}}(M)\simeq\operatorname{Sh}_{\Lambda_{1}}(M)
automatically implies that
\operatorname{Sh}_{\Lambda_{0}}^{cpt}(M)\simeq\operatorname{Sh}_{\Lambda_{1}}^{cpt}(M)
and
\operatorname{Sh}_{\Lambda_{0}}^{pp}(M)\simeq\operatorname{Sh}_{\Lambda_{1}}^{pp}(M)
, see Appendix
A
.

B.3.2.
Sheaf quantization of exact Lagrangians

Given an exact Lagrangian filling
L\subset T^{*}M
of a Legendrian submanifold
\Lambda\subset T^{*,\infty}M
, the theorem we are going to state defines a fully faithful functor
\operatorname{Loc}(L)\hookrightarrow\operatorname{Sh}_{\Lambda}(M)
, which realizes exact Lagrangian fillings, endowed with local systems, as objects in the constructible sheaf category.

Lemma B.19
.

Let
L_{t}\subset T^{*}M
,
t\in\mathbb{D}^{k}
, be a family of exact Lagrangian fillings of a family of Legendrian submanifold
\Lambda_{t}\subset T^{*,\infty}M
,
t\in\mathbb{D}^{k}
. Then
L_{t}
is Hamiltonian isotopic to a family of exact Lagrangians
L_{t}^{\prime}
whose primitive
f_{L_{t}^{\prime}}
is proper and bounded from below (where
\lambda_{\text{st}}|_{L_{t}^{\prime}}=df_{L_{t}^{\prime}}
).

Proof.

The proof is close to the non-parametric version of the lemma in
[
39
]
*Section 3.6. Since
L_{t}
,
t\in\mathbb{D}^{k}
, is a family of exact Lagrangian fillings of
\Lambda_{t}
,
t\in\mathbb{D}^{k}
, for any
t\in\mathbb{D}^{k}
there is some
r_{t}\gg 0
sufficiently large such that
L_{t}\cap\{(x,\xi)\in T^{*}M||\xi|>r_{t}\}=\Lambda\times(r_{t},+\infty)
. Since
D^{k}
is compact one can find
r_{0}\gg 0
such that for any
t\in D^{k}
,
L_{t}\cap\{(x,\xi)\in T^{*}M||\xi|>r_{0}\}=\Lambda\times(r_{0},+\infty)
.

Let
\beta:[0,+\infty)\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
be a function such that
\beta(r)=0
when
r
is sufficiently small and
\beta(r)=-1/r
when
r>r_{0}
is sufficiently large, and the Hamiltonian
H(r)=\beta(r)
. Then consider
L_{t}^{\prime}=\varphi^{\epsilon}_{H}(L_{t})
. One can check that when
r>r_{0}
,
df_{L_{t}^{\prime}}=\epsilon/r
and thus are proper and bounded from below.
∎

Theorem B.20

(
[
33
]
;
[
39
]
)
.

Let
L\subset T^{*}M
be an exact Lagrangian filling of a Legendrian submanifold
\Lambda\subset T^{*,\infty}M
with zero Maslov class, whose primitive
f_{L}
is proper and bounded from below (where
\lambda_{\text{st}}|_{L}=df_{L}
). Let
\widetilde{L}\subset J^{1}(M)\cong T^{*,\infty}_{\tau<0}(M\times\mathbb{R})
be the Legendrian lift of
L
. Then

(1)

there exists a fully faithful functor
\Psi_{\widetilde{L}}:\operatorname{Loc}_{\epsilon}(\mathcal{I}_{\widetilde{L}})\rightarrow\operatorname{Sh}_{\widetilde{L}}(M\times\mathbb{R})
such that the stalk at
M\times\{-\infty\}
is acyclic, and
m_{\widetilde{L}}\circ\Psi_{\widetilde{L}}\simeq\mathrm{id}
;

(2)

the proper push forward functor
\pi_{M,!}:\operatorname{Sh}_{\widetilde{L}}(M\times\mathbb{R})\rightarrow\operatorname{Sh}_{\Lambda}(M)
via the projection
\pi_{M}:M\times\mathbb{R}\rightarrow M
is fully faithful, and for
\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}\in\operatorname{Sh}_{\widetilde{L}}(M\times\mathbb{R})
,

\pi_{M,!}\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}=i_{M\times\{+\infty\}}^{-1}j_{M\times\mathbb{R},*}\widetilde{\mathcal{F}},

where
i_{M\times\{+\infty\}}:M\times\{+\infty\}\hookrightarrow M\times[-\infty,+\infty]
and
j_{M\times\mathbb{R}}:M\times\mathbb{R}\hookrightarrow M\times[-\infty,+\infty]
are the inclusions.

Proof.

The proof appropriately modifies the arguments from
[
39
]
*Section 3. The differences from
[
39
]
are that (i) we are considering exact Lagrangians with primitives bounded from below instead of the ones bounded from above, and (ii) we are using the proper push forward
\pi_{M!}
in Step (2) instead of the standard push forward
\pi_{M*}
.

Firstly, Step (1) is the same as in
[
39
]
. Namely, we use the equivalence
\operatorname{Loc}_{\epsilon}(\mathcal{I}_{\widetilde{L}})\simeq\mu\operatorname{Sh}_{\widetilde{L}}(\widetilde{L})
to obtain a microlocal sheaf
\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}_{\mu}\in\mu\operatorname{Sh}_{\widetilde{L}}(\widetilde{L})
. Then consider S. Guillermou’s convolution functor to obtain the doubling sheaf
\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}_{\text{dbl},c}\in\operatorname{Sh}_{\widetilde{L}\cup T_{c}(\widetilde{L})}(M\times\mathbb{R})
when
c>0
is small. Since
\widetilde{L}
has no Reeb chords, for any
c,c^{\prime}>0
we have an equivalence induced by Hamiltonian isotopy
35
35

35

Strictly speaking, non-existence of Reeb chords only implies the existence of a Legendrian isotopy from
\widetilde{L}\cup T_{c}(\widetilde{L})
to
\widetilde{L}\cup T_{c^{\prime}}(\widetilde{L})
. Since the corresponding Hamiltonian is not compactly supported, it is not clear that the integrated flow exists. In our setting this can be verified and is explained in
[
39
]
*Section 3.14.

T_{c,c^{\prime}}:\operatorname{Sh}_{\widetilde{L}\cup T_{c}(\widetilde{L})}(M\times\mathbb{R})\xrightarrow{\sim}\operatorname{Sh}_{\widetilde{L}\cup T_{c^{\prime}}(\widetilde{L})}(M\times\mathbb{R});\,\,\,T_{c,c^{\prime}}(\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}_{\text{dbl},c})=\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}_{\text{dbl},c^{\prime}}.

Choose
C^{\prime}\gg C\gg 0
such that (i)
T^{*,\infty}(M\times(-\infty,C))\cap T_{C^{\prime}}(\widetilde{L})=\emptyset
, and (ii) there exists a diffeomorphism
\varphi_{C}:M\times(-\infty,+\infty)\xrightarrow{\sim}M\times(-\infty,C)
such that
\varphi_{C}^{*}:T^{*,\infty}(M\times(-\infty,C))\xrightarrow{\sim}T^{*,\infty}(M\times\mathbb{R})
sends
\widetilde{L}\cap T^{*,\infty}_{\tau<0}(M\times(-\infty,C))
to
\widetilde{L}
. Write
j_{C}:M\times(-\infty,C)\hookrightarrow M\times\mathbb{R}
for the inclusion map. Then for
C^{\prime}\gg C
,

\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}=\varphi_{C}^{-1}(j_{C}^{-1}\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}_{\text{dbl},C^{\prime}})\in\operatorname{Sh}_{\widetilde{L}}(M\times\mathbb{R}).

Finally, since the stratification of
M\times\mathbb{R}
determined by the front projection of
\widetilde{L}
extends to a stratification of
M\times[-\infty,+\infty]
, we can get a sheaf

j_{\mathbb{R}*}\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}\in\operatorname{Sh}_{\widetilde{L}}(M\times[-\infty,+\infty]).

In Step (2), there are some differences with
[
39
]
(see their Section 3.18) when showing full faithfulness, in our case the argument is as follows. Fix two sheaves
\widetilde{\mathcal{F}},\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}\in\operatorname{Sh}_{\widetilde{L}}(M\times\mathbb{R})
. By adjunction we have

Hom(\pi_{M!}\widetilde{\mathcal{F}},\pi_{M!}\widetilde{\mathcal{G}})\simeq Hom(\widetilde{\mathcal{F}},\pi_{M}^{!}\pi_{M!}\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}).

Choose
a^{\prime}\gg a\gg 0
. Consider a Hamiltonian flow
\varphi_{H}
on
T^{*,\infty}_{\tau<0}(M\times\mathbb{R})
that carries
(\Lambda\times(-a,+\infty))\cup(\widetilde{L}\cap T^{*,\infty}(M\times(-a,+\infty)))
to
(T_{-a^{\prime}}(\widetilde{L})\cup\widetilde{L})\cap T^{*,\infty}(M\times(-a,+\infty))
. Write
j_{a}:M\times(-a,+\infty)\rightarrow M\times\mathbb{R}
for the inclusion, and
\pi_{M,a}:M\times(-a,+\infty)\rightarrow M
the projection. Since
T_{-a^{\prime}}(\widetilde{L})\subset T^{*,\infty}_{\tau<0}(M\times\mathbb{R})
, by microlocal Morse lemma
[
41
]
*Proposition 5.4.17 (ii) for proper push forward (instead of standard pushforward as in
[
39
]
), we know that

\Phi_{H}(\pi_{M,a}^{!}\pi_{M!}\widetilde{\mathcal{G}})\simeq\Phi_{H}(\pi_{M,a}^{!}\pi_{M,a!}j_{a}^{-1}T_{-a^{\prime}}(\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}))\simeq j_{a}^{-1}T_{-a^{\prime}}\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}.

Then by the singular support estimate
[
41
]
